Enjoy things? Um, not really. Like, I used to get super excited about going to gallery nights downtown, or playing basketball, or even just grabbing tacos from the food trucks. There's this one truck that parks near the plaza where I work, right by the shoe store. The guy who runs it always plays his radio way too loud. It's always that same station, and the bass just rattles the metal siding of the truck. I mean, the food is alright, but the price of tacos went up to like four bucks each now, which is crazy for a food truck. You'd think they'd be cheaper since they don't pay rent for an actual building, you know? The city keeps raising the permit fees though, so I guess they have to pass the cost onto us. 

But yeah, I used to love doing all that, or like, helping out the neighborhood kids with art stuff. Now... I don't know. If I try to go do any of it, I just stand there feeling completely flat. Nothing really clicks in my head anymore. I usually just make up some excuse to leave early.

It's always that same station, and the bass just rattles the metal siding of the truck. I mean, the food is alright, but the price of tacos went up to like four bucks each now, which is crazy for a food truck. You'd think they'd be cheaper since they don't pay rent for an actual building, you know? The city keeps raising the permit fees though, so I guess they have to pass the cost onto us. \n\nBut yeah, I used to love doing all that, or like, helping out the neighborhood kids with art stuff. Now... I don't know. If I try to go do any of it, I just stand there feeling completely flat. Nothing really clicks in my head anymore. I usually just make up some excuse to leave early.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: How long? Um... I guess it's been about two months now. Yeah, right around the time I won that window display award at work. The district manager came down and made this whole big deal about it, taking pictures and everything. He was wearing this really weird tie, actually—it had little golf clubs all over it, but they were neon green. Who wears neon green golf clubs on a tie? And he kept checking his phone the whole time, like every two minutes, which was super distracting. \n\nHe drives this massive SUV, one of those luxury ones that takes up two parking spots, and he always parks right in front of the store where the customers are supposed to go. My manager never says anything to him about it because, well, he's the district manager. But I had to haul a bunch of empty cardboard boxes out to the recycling bin that day, and I had to squeeze right past his stupid car. The recycling bin was completely overflowing anyway because the truck didn't come on Monday like it usually does. I heard the driver called out sick or something. \n\nBut yeah, ever since that award thing and all the attention from him, that's when all this started. It's been about two months.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're empty or hollow inside most of the time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 25 / retail_salesperson / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Empty? Yeah, I guess... I mean, I don't really know if empty is the right word, but I don't really feel much of anything. It's just kind of blank, you know? Like when you turn on the TV and the cable is out, and it's just that blue screen. My cable actually went out last weekend right in the middle of a movie I was trying to watch. It was one of those old action movies from the 90s, the ones with the really bad special effects where the explosions look totally fake. 

I tried calling the cable company, but they put me on hold for like forty-five minutes. And their hold music is terrible. It's this weird smooth jazz loop that repeats every thirty seconds. I swear I've heard that same saxophone riff a thousand times. The guy finally answered and said there was an outage in my area because a tree branch fell on a line over on Elm Street. Which is crazy because the city was just out there trimming trees last month. They blocked off the whole street for two days and I had to take a detour to work that added like twenty minutes to my drive. 

But, um... yeah. I don't know if I feel empty, exactly. Just... not really feeling anything.

It was one of those old action movies from the 90s, the ones with the really bad special effects where the explosions look totally fake. \n\nI tried calling the cable company, but they put me on hold for like forty-five minutes. And their hold music is terrible. It's this weird smooth jazz loop that repeats every thirty seconds. I swear I've heard that same saxophone riff a thousand times. The guy finally answered and said there was an outage in my area because a tree branch fell on a line over on Elm Street. Which is crazy because the city was just out there trimming trees last month. They blocked off the whole street for two days and I had to take a detour to work that added like twenty minutes to my drive. \n\nBut, um... yeah. I don't know if I feel empty, exactly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Down or depressed? I'm not really sure. I don't think I've felt sad or anything like that. I don't really keep track of my days like that anyway, especially with my schedule at the store being all over the place. Like this week, they have me doing three closing shifts and then a random morning open on Thursday. Which makes no sense because Thursday is truck day, and usually Gary does the morning on truck day because he knows the delivery guy. 

The delivery guy is this huge dude named Marcus who always brings his dog with him in the cab. It's a pitbull mix, I think, really sweet, but it sheds everywhere. I actually had to lend Marcus a lint roller once because his uniform was just completely covered in white dog hair. We sell those lint rollers right by the register, but they're the cheap kind where the paper tears diagonally when you try to peel off the top layer, and then you just end up wasting half the roll trying to get a clean sheet. It's so annoying when companies make stuff cheap like that just to save a few cents. 

But yeah, I don't know about feeling down for most of the day. I haven't really noticed feeling sad, just... blank, like I said.

It was one of those old action movies from the 90s, the ones with the really bad special effects where the explosions look totally fake. \n\nI tried calling the cable company, but they put me on hold for like forty-five minutes. Like this week, they have me doing three closing shifts and then a random morning open on Thursday. Which makes no sense because Thursday is truck day, and usually Gary does the morning on truck day because he knows the delivery guy. \n\nThe delivery guy is this huge dude named Marcus who always brings his dog with him in the cab. It's a pitbull mix, I think, really sweet, but it sheds everywhere. I actually had to lend Marcus a lint roller once because his uniform was just completely covered in white dog hair. We sell those lint rollers right by the register, but they're the cheap kind where the paper tears diagonally when you try to peel off the top layer, and then you just end up wasting half the roll trying to get a clean sheet. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Pleasure? Um... no, not really. I mean, I used to. I used to get really excited about going to gallery nights downtown, or playing pickup basketball on the weekends. Sometimes we'd go grab tacos from this food truck that parks near the courts. They make these amazing al pastor tacos with pineapple on top. But the last time I went, the guy running the truck gave me the wrong change. He handed me back a five instead of a ten, and when I tried to tell him, he got all defensive about it. He had this really loud generator running right behind the truck, so we were basically just yelling at each other over the noise. 

Those generators are so bad for the environment anyway, just pumping out exhaust right where people are trying to eat. The city was supposed to pass some ordinance about food trucks having to use quieter, cleaner power sources, but I guess it got stalled in city council. My neighbor's brother actually works for the city planning office, and he says nothing ever gets done because they argue over the budget for months. He's always complaining about it when he comes over for their Sunday barbecues. They use one of those cheap charcoal grills that always smells like lighter fluid, and the smoke blows right into my bedroom window if I leave it open. 

But yeah, even when I try to do stuff I used to like, like helping the neighborhood kids with their art projects, I just stand there feeling totally flat. I don't get anything out of it anymore. I usually just end up making up some excuse to leave early.

We sell those lint rollers right by the register, but they're the cheap kind where the paper tears diagonally when you try to peel off the top layer, and then you just end up wasting half the roll trying to get a clean sheet. It's so annoying when companies make stuff cheap like that just to save a few cents. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know about feeling down for most of the day. I haven't really noticed feeling sad, just... blank, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in doing?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Um... no, not really. I mean, I used to. I used to get really excited about going to gallery nights downtown, or playing pickup basketball on the weekends. Sometimes we'd go grab tacos from this food truck that parks near the courts. They make these amazing al pastor tacos with pineapple on top. But the last time I went, the guy running the truck gave me the wrong change. He handed me back a five instead of a ten, and when I tried to tell him, he got all defensive about it. He had this really loud generator running right behind the truck, so we were basically just yelling at each other over the noise. \n\nThose generators are so bad for the environment anyway, just pumping out exhaust right where people are trying to eat. The city was supposed to pass some ordinance about food trucks having to use quieter, cleaner power sources, but I guess it got stalled in city council. My neighbor's brother actually works for the city planning office, and he says nothing ever gets done because they argue over the budget for months. He's always complaining about it when he comes over for their Sunday barbecues. They use one of those cheap charcoal grills that always smells like lighter fluid, and the smoke blows right into my bedroom window if I leave it open. \n\nBut yeah, even when I try to do stuff I used to like, like helping the neighborhood kids with their art projects, I just stand there feeling totally flat. I don't get anything out of it anymore. I usually just end up making up some excuse to leave early.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any small things that still give you some joy or pleasure?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Like I was just saying, man, not even the small things. I used to get really hyped about all that stuff—the gallery nights, basketball, even just grabbing those tacos or helping the kids on my street with their art projects. But now... I don't know, I just stand around feeling completely flat. I usually just end up making up some excuse to leave early. 

Actually, the last time I made an excuse to leave, I told them I had to go meet my landlord because he was finally fixing the front door of my apartment building. Which he did do, eventually, but he painted it this really ugly yellow color. He told me it was supposed to be "sunflower," but it looks way more like mustard. And he used the cheap paint, the kind that peels off if you look at it wrong. He didn't even put down a tarp, so there are these yellow paint splatters all over the concrete steps now. 

The mailman actually slipped on one of the wet spots the next day and dropped a whole stack of letters right into a puddle. I saw him trying to wipe off a magazine with his sleeve, but it was totally ruined. It was one of those car magazines, the ones with the really glossy pages that just instantly stick together when they get wet. My uncle used to collect those back in the day. He had a whole garage full of them stacked up next to his old Chevy that he never finished restoring. 

But, uh, yeah... to answer your question, no. No joy or pleasure from the small stuff either. Just flat.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, I guess it's been going on for about two months now. Yeah, right around the time I got that window display award at work and the district manager started paying so much attention to me. She came in specifically to shake my hand, which was weird because she usually just walks right past the sales floor and goes straight to the back office. The back office is always freezing, by the way. The thermostat is locked behind this little plastic box, and the key has been missing since before I even started working there. My coworker Terry tried to pick the lock with a paperclip once, but he just ended up breaking the paperclip inside the keyhole. Terry thinks he's this master lockpicker because he watches all these videos online, but he can't even get his own locker open half the time. He actually had to get the mall security guard to cut his padlock off last week because he forgot his combination again. The security guard was this older guy who looked like he was about to retire, and he had these huge bolt cutters that looked like they weighed more than he did. He said he used to work construction back in the eighties, building those big shopping centers out in the suburbs that are all empty now. 

But yeah, about two months, ever since all that stuff with the award and the district manager.

I'm just throwing it all in the trash.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Sad? Uh, I don't know. I haven't really noticed feeling sad, honestly. There's this one truck that parks near the plaza where I work, right by the shoe store. The guy who runs it always plays his radio way too loud. It's always that same station, and the bass just rattles the metal siding of the truck. I mean, the food is alright, but the price of tacos went up to like four bucks each now, which is crazy for a food truck. You'd think they'd be cheaper since they don't pay rent for an actual building, you know? The city keeps raising the permit fees though, so I guess they have to pass the cost onto us. \n\nBut yeah, I used to love doing all that, or like, helping out the neighborhood kids with art stuff. Now... I don't know. If I try to go do any of it, I just stand there feeling completely flat. Nothing really clicks in my head anymore. I usually just make up some excuse to leave early.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: How long? Um... I guess it's been about two months now. Yeah, right around the time I won that window display award at work. The district manager came down and made this whole big deal about it, taking pictures and everything. He was wearing this really weird tie, actually—it had little golf clubs all over it, but they were neon green. Who wears neon green golf clubs on a tie? And he kept checking his phone the whole time, like every two minutes, which was super distracting. \n\nHe drives this massive SUV, one of those luxury ones that takes up two parking spots, and he always parks right in front of the store where the customers are supposed to go. My manager never says anything to him about it because, well, he's the district manager. But I had to haul a bunch of empty cardboard boxes out to the recycling bin that day, and I had to squeeze right past his stupid car. The recycling bin was completely overflowing anyway because the truck didn't come on Monday like it usually does. I heard the driver called out sick or something. \n\nBut yeah, ever since that award thing and all the attention from him, that's when all this started. It's been about two months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're empty or hollow inside most of the time?\n\nPatient: Empty? Yeah, I guess... I mean, I don't really know if empty is the right word, but I don't really feel much of anything. It's just kind of blank, you know? Like when you turn on the TV and the cable is out, and it's just that blue screen. My cable actually went out last weekend right in the middle of a movie I was trying to watch. It was one of those old action movies from the 90s, the ones with the really bad special effects where the explosions look totally fake. \n\nI tried calling the cable company, but they put me on hold for like forty-five minutes. And their hold music is terrible. It's this weird smooth jazz loop that repeats every thirty seconds. I swear I've heard that same saxophone riff a thousand times. The guy finally answered and said there was an outage in my area because a tree branch fell on a line over on Elm Street. Which is crazy because the city was just out there trimming trees last month. They blocked off the whole street for two days and I had to take a detour to work that added like twenty minutes to my drive. \n\nBut, um... yeah. I don't know if I feel empty, exactly. Just... not really feeling anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two months where you felt really down or depressed for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Down or depressed? I'm not really sure. I don't think I've felt sad or anything like that. I don't really keep track of my days like that anyway, especially with my schedule at the store being all over the place. Like this week, they have me doing three closing shifts and then a random morning open on Thursday. Which makes no sense because Thursday is truck day, and usually Gary does the morning on truck day because he knows the delivery guy. \n\nThe delivery guy is this huge dude named Marcus who always brings his dog with him in the cab. It's a pitbull mix, I think, really sweet, but it sheds everywhere. I actually had to lend Marcus a lint roller once because his uniform was just completely covered in white dog hair. We sell those lint rollers right by the register, but they're the cheap kind where the paper tears diagonally when you try to peel off the top layer, and then you just end up wasting half the roll trying to get a clean sheet. It's so annoying when companies make stuff cheap like that just to save a few cents. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know about feeling down for most of the day. I haven't really noticed feeling sad, just... blank, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in doing?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Um... no, not really. I mean, I used to. I used to get really excited about going to gallery nights downtown, or playing pickup basketball on the weekends. Sometimes we'd go grab tacos from this food truck that parks near the courts. They make these amazing al pastor tacos with pineapple on top. But the last time I went, the guy running the truck gave me the wrong change. He handed me back a five instead of a ten, and when I tried to tell him, he got all defensive about it. He had this really loud generator running right behind the truck, so we were basically just yelling at each other over the noise. \n\nThose generators are so bad for the environment anyway, just pumping out exhaust right where people are trying to eat. The city was supposed to pass some ordinance about food trucks having to use quieter, cleaner power sources, but I guess it got stalled in city council. My neighbor's brother actually works for the city planning office, and he says nothing ever gets done because they argue over the budget for months. He's always complaining about it when he comes over for their Sunday barbecues. They use one of those cheap charcoal grills that always smells like lighter fluid, and the smoke blows right into my bedroom window if I leave it open. \n\nBut yeah, even when I try to do stuff I used to like, like helping the neighborhood kids with their art projects, I just stand there feeling totally flat. I don't get anything out of it anymore. I usually just end up making up some excuse to leave early.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still give you some joy or pleasure?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, man, not even the small things. I used to get really hyped about all that stuff—the gallery nights, basketball, even just grabbing those tacos or helping the kids on my street with their art projects. But now... I don't know, I just stand around feeling completely flat. I usually just end up making up some excuse to leave early. \n\nActually, the last time I made an excuse to leave, I told them I had to go meet my landlord because he was finally fixing the front door of my apartment building. Which he did do, eventually, but he painted it this really ugly yellow color. He told me it was supposed to be \"sunflower,\" but it looks way more like mustard. And he used the cheap paint, the kind that peels off if you look at it wrong. He didn't even put down a tarp, so there are these yellow paint splatters all over the concrete steps now. \n\nThe mailman actually slipped on one of the wet spots the next day and dropped a whole stack of letters right into a puddle. I saw him trying to wipe off a magazine with his sleeve, but it was totally ruined. It was one of those car magazines, the ones with the really glossy pages that just instantly stick together when they get wet. My uncle used to collect those back in the day. He had a whole garage full of them stacked up next to his old Chevy that he never finished restoring. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... to answer your question, no. No joy or pleasure from the small stuff either. Just flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, that's exactly what I'm trying to say. Even just getting those al pastor tacos from the food truck or helping the neighbor kids with their little art projects. I used to really look forward to all of it. But lately, I just... I don't care. I show up and just stand there feeling completely flat, like there's nothing there. I usually just end up making up some excuse to leave early. \n\nLike last time I was at the courts, I told the guys I had to go because my phone battery was dying and I needed to call my mom. Which wasn't even true, my phone was at like eighty percent. But it's an older phone, one of those models where the battery drains super fast if it gets cold. I actually dropped it in the snow last winter when I was waiting for the bus, and it took me like twenty minutes to find it because I had a white case on it. I don't know why they make white phone cases, it's just asking for trouble. My buddy at the store has this bright orange case that's supposedly military-grade, like you can run it over with a car. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. It's just... everyday is the same flat feeling. Like I said, I used to get so excited about those gallery nights downtown, or playing basketball, grabbing those food-truck tacos, or even just helping the kids on my street with their art stuff. But now, it doesn't matter what day it is, I just stand there feeling completely flat and end up making an excuse to leave. 

Actually, the last time I was helping the kids with their art stuff, we were using this glitter glue. And I don't know who invented glitter, but that stuff gets everywhere. It's like sand from the beach. You go to the beach once, and you're finding sand in your shoes for the next three months. My cousin took me to the beach down in Florida a few years ago, and we rented this little convertible. It was red, bright red. But the AC was broken, so we had to drive with the top down the whole time, which was fine until it started pouring rain out of nowhere. You know how it rains in Florida, just suddenly the sky falls. We had to pull over under this overpass and wait it out next to this guy selling boiled peanuts out of the back of his truck. He kept trying to offer us some, but they smelled like wet cardboard. I don't get the appeal of boiled peanuts, honestly. They're just soggy. 

But yeah, to answer your question, no. I don't have good days or bad days with it. I just feel flat all the time and want to leave.

I'm not really sure. I don't think I've felt sad or anything like that. I don't really keep track of my days like that anyway, especially with my schedule at the store being all over the place. Like this week, they have me doing three closing shifts and then a random morning open on Thursday. Which makes no sense because Thursday is truck day, and usually Gary does the morning on truck day because he knows the delivery guy. \n\nThe delivery guy is this huge dude named Marcus who always brings his dog with him in the cab. But now, it doesn't matter what day it is, I just stand there feeling completely flat and end up making an excuse to leave. \n\nActually, the last time I was helping the kids with their art stuff, we were using this glitter glue. And I don't know who invented glitter, but that stuff gets everywhere. It's like sand from the beach. You go to the beach once, and you're finding sand in your shoes for the next three months. My cousin took me to the beach down in Florida a few years ago, and we rented this little convertible. It was red, bright red. But the AC was broken, so we had to drive with the top down the whole time, which was fine until it started pouring rain out of nowhere. You know how it rains in Florida, just suddenly the sky falls. We had to pull over under this overpass and wait it out next to this guy selling boiled peanuts out of the back of his truck. He kept trying to offer us some, but they smelled like wet cardboard. I don't get the appeal of boiled peanuts, honestly. They're just soggy. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question, no. I don't have good days or bad days with it. I just feel flat all the time and want to leave.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like this flat feeling comes and goes, or is it always there?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 25 / retail_salesperson / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"I used to get excited about gallery nights, playing basketball, grabbing food-truck tacos, or helping kids with art stuff, but lately I just stand there feeling flat and usually make an excuse to leave.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It's always there. Like I was just saying, it really doesn't matter what I'm doing. The gallery nights, the pickup games, getting those food-truck tacos, or the art stuff with the kids... I used to get so excited for all of that. But now it's just a constant flat feeling, and I just stand there until I can make up an excuse to leave. 

I actually used a really stupid excuse at the food truck the other day. I told the guy I had to go because I left my oven on. Who even says that anymore? But I just had to get out of there. The guy who runs the truck, Hector, he always parks by this intersection where the streetlights are constantly flickering. It's like a strobe light, it drives me crazy. The city never fixes anything around here. My coworker Terry tried to call the city helpline about this massive pothole right outside the mall, like, a literal crater. People were losing hubcaps in it left and right. Terry was on hold for almost two hours and they just played this really loud, distorted jazz music the whole time. He actually started memorizing the saxophone solos because the track just kept looping over and over. 

But yeah, anyway... no, it doesn't come and go. It's just always flat.

They blocked off the whole street for two days and I had to take a detour to work that added like twenty minutes to my drive. \n\nBut, um... yeah. I don't know if I feel empty, exactly. Just... not really feeling anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two months where you felt really down or depressed for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Down or depressed? I'm not really sure. I don't think I've felt sad or anything like that. I don't really keep track of my days like that anyway, especially with my schedule at the store being all over the place. Like this week, they have me doing three closing shifts and then a random morning open on Thursday. Which makes no sense because Thursday is truck day, and usually Gary does the morning on truck day because he knows the delivery guy. \n\nThe delivery guy is this huge dude named Marcus who always brings his dog with him in the cab. It's a pitbull mix, I think, really sweet, but it sheds everywhere. I actually had to lend Marcus a lint roller once because his uniform was just completely covered in white dog hair. We sell those lint rollers right by the register, but they're the cheap kind where the paper tears diagonally when you try to peel off the top layer, and then you just end up wasting half the roll trying to get a clean sheet. I haven't really noticed feeling sad, just... blank, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in doing?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Um... no, not really. I mean, I used to. I used to get really excited about going to gallery nights downtown, or playing pickup basketball on the weekends. Sometimes we'd go grab tacos from this food truck that parks near the courts. They make these amazing al pastor tacos with pineapple on top. But the last time I went, the guy running the truck gave me the wrong change. He handed me back a five instead of a ten, and when I tried to tell him, he got all defensive about it. The city was supposed to pass some ordinance about food trucks having to use quieter, cleaner power sources, but I guess it got stalled in city council. My neighbor's brother actually works for the city planning office, and he says nothing ever gets done because they argue over the budget for months. He's always complaining about it when he comes over for their Sunday barbecues. They use one of those cheap charcoal grills that always smells like lighter fluid, and the smoke blows right into my bedroom window if I leave it open. \n\nBut yeah, even when I try to do stuff I used to like, like helping the neighborhood kids with their art projects, I just stand there feeling totally flat. I don't get anything out of it anymore. I usually just end up making up some excuse to leave early.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still give you some joy or pleasure?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, man, not even the small things. I used to get really hyped about all that stuff—the gallery nights, basketball, even just grabbing those tacos or helping the kids on my street with their art projects. But now... I don't know, I just stand around feeling completely flat. I usually just end up making up some excuse to leave early. \n\nActually, the last time I made an excuse to leave, I told them I had to go meet my landlord because he was finally fixing the front door of my apartment building. Which he did do, eventually, but he painted it this really ugly yellow color. He told me it was supposed to be \"sunflower,\" but it looks way more like mustard. And he used the cheap paint, the kind that peels off if you look at it wrong. He didn't even put down a tarp, so there are these yellow paint splatters all over the concrete steps now. \n\nThe mailman actually slipped on one of the wet spots the next day and dropped a whole stack of letters right into a puddle. I saw him trying to wipe off a magazine with his sleeve, but it was totally ruined. It was one of those car magazines, the ones with the really glossy pages that just instantly stick together when they get wet. My uncle used to collect those back in the day. He had a whole garage full of them stacked up next to his old Chevy that he never finished restoring. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... to answer your question, no. No joy or pleasure from the small stuff either. Just flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, that's exactly what I'm trying to say. I used to be all about those gallery nights downtown, or getting a pickup game going on the weekend. Even just getting those al pastor tacos from the food truck or helping the neighbor kids with their little art projects. I used to really look forward to all of it. But lately, I just... I don't care. I show up and just stand there feeling completely flat, like there's nothing there. I usually just end up making up some excuse to leave early. \n\nLike last time I was at the courts, I told the guys I had to go because my phone battery was dying and I needed to call my mom. Which wasn't even true, my phone was at like eighty percent. But it's an older phone, one of those models where the battery drains super fast if it gets cold. I actually dropped it in the snow last winter when I was waiting for the bus, and it took me like twenty minutes to find it because I had a white case on it. I don't know why they make white phone cases, it's just asking for trouble. My buddy at the store has this bright orange case that's supposedly military-grade, like you can run it over with a car. He actually tried to prove it once in the parking lot, but he missed the phone and backed into one of those concrete light poles instead. Totally dented his bumper. He tried to pop it back out with a plunger, which I saw on a YouTube video once, but it didn't work at all. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I don't have any interest in doing those things anymore. The back office is always freezing, by the way. The thermostat is locked behind this little plastic box, and the key has been missing since before I even started working there. My coworker Terry tried to pick the lock with a paperclip once, but he just ended up breaking the paperclip inside the keyhole. Terry thinks he's this master lockpicker because he watches all these videos online, but he can't even get his own locker open half the time. He actually had to get the mall security guard to cut his padlock off last week because he forgot his combination again. The security guard was this older guy who looked like he was about to retire, and he had these huge bolt cutters that looked like they weighed more than he did. He said he used to work construction back in the eighties, building those big shopping centers out in the suburbs that are all empty now. \n\nBut yeah, about two months, ever since all that stuff with the award and the district manager.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you feel more interested in things than others?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. He had a whole garage full of them stacked up next to his old Chevy that he never finished restoring. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... to answer your question, no. No joy or pleasure from the small stuff either. Just flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, that's exactly what I'm trying to say. I used to be all about those gallery nights downtown, or getting a pickup game going on the weekend. Even just getting those al pastor tacos from the food truck or helping the neighbor kids with their little art projects. I used to really look forward to all of it. But lately, I just... I don't care. I show up and just stand there feeling completely flat, like there's nothing there. I usually just end up making up some excuse to leave early. \n\nLike last time I was at the courts, I told the guys I had to go because my phone battery was dying and I needed to call my mom. Which wasn't even true, my phone was at like eighty percent. But it's an older phone, one of those models where the battery drains super fast if it gets cold. I actually dropped it in the snow last winter when I was waiting for the bus, and it took me like twenty minutes to find it because I had a white case on it. I don't know why they make white phone cases, it's just asking for trouble. My buddy at the store has this bright orange case that's supposedly military-grade, like you can run it over with a car. He actually tried to prove it once in the parking lot, but he missed the phone and backed into one of those concrete light poles instead. Totally dented his bumper. He tried to pop it back out with a plunger, which I saw on a YouTube video once, but it didn't work at all. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I don't have any interest in doing those things anymore. I just want to go home.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess it's been going on for about two months now. Yeah, right around the time I got that window display award at work and the district manager started paying so much attention to me. She came in specifically to shake my hand, which was weird because she usually just walks right past the sales floor and goes straight to the back office. The back office is always freezing, by the way. The thermostat is locked behind this little plastic box, and the key has been missing since before I even started working there. My coworker Terry tried to pick the lock with a paperclip once, but he just ended up breaking the paperclip inside the keyhole. Terry thinks he's this master lockpicker because he watches all these videos online, but he can't even get his own locker open half the time. He actually had to get the mall security guard to cut his padlock off last week because he forgot his combination again. The security guard was this older guy who looked like he was about to retire, and he had these huge bolt cutters that looked like they weighed more than he did. He said he used to work construction back in the eighties, building those big shopping centers out in the suburbs that are all empty now. \n\nBut yeah, about two months, ever since all that stuff with the award and the district manager.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you feel more interested in things than others?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It's just... everyday is the same flat feeling. Like I said, I used to get so excited about those gallery nights downtown, or playing basketball, grabbing those food-truck tacos, or even just helping the kids on my street with their art stuff. And I don't know who invented glitter, but that stuff gets everywhere. It's like sand from the beach. You go to the beach once, and you're finding sand in your shoes for the next three months. My cousin took me to the beach down in Florida a few years ago, and we rented this little convertible. It was red, bright red. But the AC was broken, so we had to drive with the top down the whole time, which was fine until it started pouring rain out of nowhere. You know how it rains in Florida, just suddenly the sky falls. We had to pull over under this overpass and wait it out next to this guy selling boiled peanuts out of the back of his truck. He kept trying to offer us some, but they smelled like wet cardboard. I don't get the appeal of boiled peanuts, honestly. They're just soggy. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question, no. I don't have good days or bad days with it. I just feel flat all the time and want to leave.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this flat feeling comes and goes, or is it always there?\n\nPatient: It's always there. Yeah, man, like I keep saying... every single day. It's exactly the same. It's honestly like Groundhog Day. Did you ever see that movie? We watched it in my ninth-grade English class, right before I stopped going to school. My teacher, Mr. Peterson, he was completely obsessed with Bill Murray. It broke the second he plugged it in, just started smoking in his living room. It smelled like burnt hair in his apartment for a week. 

But anyway, yeah, I'm pretty sure I already told you, but there's no variation at all. Whether it's shooting hoops, going to the downtown galleries, getting those tacos, or doing the art projects with the neighborhood kids... I don't get excited about a single bit of it anymore. I just stand around feeling totally empty until I can sneak off and go home.

It's always that same station, and the bass just rattles the metal siding of the truck. I mean, the food is alright, but the price of tacos went up to like four bucks each now, which is crazy for a food truck. I usually just make up some excuse to leave early.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: How long? Um... I guess it's been about two months now. Yeah, right around the time I won that window display award at work. The district manager came down and made this whole big deal about it, taking pictures and everything. He was wearing this really weird tie, actually—it had little golf clubs all over it, but they were neon green. Who wears neon green golf clubs on a tie? And he kept checking his phone the whole time, like every two minutes, which was super distracting. \n\nHe drives this massive SUV, one of those luxury ones that takes up two parking spots, and he always parks right in front of the store where the customers are supposed to go. My manager never says anything to him about it because, well, he's the district manager. The recycling bin was completely overflowing anyway because the truck didn't come on Monday like it usually does. I heard the driver called out sick or something. \n\nBut yeah, ever since that award thing and all the attention from him, that's when all this started. It's been about two months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're empty or hollow inside most of the time?\n\nPatient: Empty? Yeah, I guess... I mean, I don't really know if empty is the right word, but I don't really feel much of anything. It's just kind of blank, you know? Like when you turn on the TV and the cable is out, and it's just that blue screen. My cable actually went out last weekend right in the middle of a movie I was trying to watch. It was one of those old action movies from the 90s, the ones with the really bad special effects where the explosions look totally fake. \n\nI tried calling the cable company, but they put me on hold for like forty-five minutes. They blocked off the whole street for two days and I had to take a detour to work that added like twenty minutes to my drive. \n\nBut, um... yeah. I don't know if I feel empty, exactly. Like this week, they have me doing three closing shifts and then a random morning open on Thursday. Which makes no sense because Thursday is truck day, and usually Gary does the morning on truck day because he knows the delivery guy. \n\nThe delivery guy is this huge dude named Marcus who always brings his dog with him in the cab. It's a pitbull mix, I think, really sweet, but it sheds everywhere. I actually had to lend Marcus a lint roller once because his uniform was just completely covered in white dog hair. We sell those lint rollers right by the register, but they're the cheap kind where the paper tears diagonally when you try to peel off the top layer, and then you just end up wasting half the roll trying to get a clean sheet. It's so annoying when companies make stuff cheap like that just to save a few cents. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know about feeling down for most of the day. I haven't really noticed feeling sad, just... blank, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in doing?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Um... no, not really. I mean, I used to. I used to get really excited about going to gallery nights downtown, or playing pickup basketball on the weekends. Sometimes we'd go grab tacos from this food truck that parks near the courts. They make these amazing al pastor tacos with pineapple on top. But the last time I went, the guy running the truck gave me the wrong change. He handed me back a five instead of a ten, and when I tried to tell him, he got all defensive about it. He had this really loud generator running right behind the truck, so we were basically just yelling at each other over the noise. \n\nThose generators are so bad for the environment anyway, just pumping out exhaust right where people are trying to eat. The city was supposed to pass some ordinance about food trucks having to use quieter, cleaner power sources, but I guess it got stalled in city council. My neighbor's brother actually works for the city planning office, and he says nothing ever gets done because they argue over the budget for months. He's always complaining about it when he comes over for their Sunday barbecues. They use one of those cheap charcoal grills that always smells like lighter fluid, and the smoke blows right into my bedroom window if I leave it open. \n\nBut yeah, even when I try to do stuff I used to like, like helping the neighborhood kids with their art projects, I just stand there feeling totally flat. I don't get anything out of it anymore. I usually just end up making up some excuse to leave early.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still give you some joy or pleasure?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, man, not even the small things. I used to get really hyped about all that stuff—the gallery nights, basketball, even just grabbing those tacos or helping the kids on my street with their art projects. But now... I don't know, I just stand around feeling completely flat. I usually just end up making up some excuse to leave early. \n\nActually, the last time I made an excuse to leave, I told them I had to go meet my landlord because he was finally fixing the front door of my apartment building. Which he did do, eventually, but he painted it this really ugly yellow color. He told me it was supposed to be \"sunflower,\" but it looks way more like mustard. And he used the cheap paint, the kind that peels off if you look at it wrong. He didn't even put down a tarp, so there are these yellow paint splatters all over the concrete steps now. \n\nThe mailman actually slipped on one of the wet spots the next day and dropped a whole stack of letters right into a puddle. I saw him trying to wipe off a magazine with his sleeve, but it was totally ruined. It was one of those car magazines, the ones with the really glossy pages that just instantly stick together when they get wet. My uncle used to collect those back in the day. He had a whole garage full of them stacked up next to his old Chevy that he never finished restoring. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... to answer your question, no. No joy or pleasure from the small stuff either. Just flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, that's exactly what I'm trying to say. I used to be all about those gallery nights downtown, or getting a pickup game going on the weekend. Even just getting those al pastor tacos from the food truck or helping the neighbor kids with their little art projects. I used to really look forward to all of it. But lately, I just... I don't care. I show up and just stand there feeling completely flat, like there's nothing there. I usually just end up making up some excuse to leave early. \n\nLike last time I was at the courts, I told the guys I had to go because my phone battery was dying and I needed to call my mom. Which wasn't even true, my phone was at like eighty percent. But it's an older phone, one of those models where the battery drains super fast if it gets cold. I actually dropped it in the snow last winter when I was waiting for the bus, and it took me like twenty minutes to find it because I had a white case on it. I don't know why they make white phone cases, it's just asking for trouble. My buddy at the store has this bright orange case that's supposedly military-grade, like you can run it over with a car. He actually tried to prove it once in the parking lot, but he missed the phone and backed into one of those concrete light poles instead. Totally dented his bumper. He tried to pop it back out with a plunger, which I saw on a YouTube video once, but it didn't work at all. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I don't have any interest in doing those things anymore. I just want to go home.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess it's been going on for about two months now. Yeah, right around the time I got that window display award at work and the district manager started paying so much attention to me. She came in specifically to shake my hand, which was weird because she usually just walks right past the sales floor and goes straight to the back office. The back office is always freezing, by the way. The thermostat is locked behind this little plastic box, and the key has been missing since before I even started working there. My coworker Terry tried to pick the lock with a paperclip once, but he just ended up breaking the paperclip inside the keyhole. Terry thinks he's this master lockpicker because he watches all these videos online, but he can't even get his own locker open half the time. He actually had to get the mall security guard to cut his padlock off last week because he forgot his combination again. The security guard was this older guy who looked like he was about to retire, and he had these huge bolt cutters that looked like they weighed more than he did. He said he used to work construction back in the eighties, building those big shopping centers out in the suburbs that are all empty now. \n\nBut yeah, about two months, ever since all that stuff with the award and the district manager.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you feel more interested in things than others?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It's just... everyday is the same flat feeling. Like I said, I used to get so excited about those gallery nights downtown, or playing basketball, grabbing those food-truck tacos, or even just helping the kids on my street with their art stuff. But now, it doesn't matter what day it is, I just stand there feeling completely flat and end up making an excuse to leave. \n\nActually, the last time I was helping the kids with their art stuff, we were using this glitter glue. And I don't know who invented glitter, but that stuff gets everywhere. It's like sand from the beach. You go to the beach once, and you're finding sand in your shoes for the next three months. My cousin took me to the beach down in Florida a few years ago, and we rented this little convertible. It was red, bright red. But the AC was broken, so we had to drive with the top down the whole time, which was fine until it started pouring rain out of nowhere. You know how it rains in Florida, just suddenly the sky falls. We had to pull over under this overpass and wait it out next to this guy selling boiled peanuts out of the back of his truck. He kept trying to offer us some, but they smelled like wet cardboard. I don't get the appeal of boiled peanuts, honestly. They're just soggy. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question, no. I don't have good days or bad days with it. I just feel flat all the time and want to leave.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this flat feeling comes and goes, or is it always there?\n\nPatient: It's always there. Like I was just saying, it really doesn't matter what I'm doing. The gallery nights, the pickup games, getting those food-truck tacos, or the art stuff with the kids... I used to get so excited for all of that. But now it's just a constant flat feeling, and I just stand there until I can make up an excuse to leave. \n\nI actually used a really stupid excuse at the food truck the other day. I told the guy I had to go because I left my oven on. Who even says that anymore? But I just had to get out of there. The guy who runs the truck, Hector, he always parks by this intersection where the streetlights are constantly flickering. It's like a strobe light, it drives me crazy. The city never fixes anything around here. My coworker Terry tried to call the city helpline about this massive pothole right outside the mall, like, a literal crater. People were losing hubcaps in it left and right. Terry was on hold for almost two hours and they just played this really loud, distorted jazz music the whole time. He actually started memorizing the saxophone solos because the track just kept looping over and over. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... no, it doesn't come and go. It's just always flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this flat feeling is something that you experience every single day without any variation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, man, like I keep saying... every single day. It's exactly the same. It's honestly like Groundhog Day. Did you ever see that movie? We watched it in my ninth-grade English class, right before I stopped going to school. My teacher, Mr. Peterson, he was completely obsessed with Bill Murray. He had this giant Ghostbusters poster right behind his desk, but it was the one from the sequel with the ghost holding up two fingers. I never really got the hype with Ghostbusters, honestly. The effects look so goofy now, like old claymation. My buddy Terry tried to argue that practical effects are way better than CGI, and he actually spent like three hundred dollars on this vintage animatronic zombie prop for Halloween just to prove a point. It broke the second he plugged it in, just started smoking in his living room. It smelled like burnt hair in his apartment for a week. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I'm pretty sure I already told you, but there's no variation at all. Whether it's shooting hoops, going to the downtown galleries, getting those tacos, or doing the art projects with the neighborhood kids... I don't get excited about a single bit of it anymore. I just stand around feeling totally empty until I can sneak off and go home.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I feel like a broken record here, doc, but no. Not even slightly better. It's just... two months, two years, it doesn't matter. It's that same flat feeling. Like I said, the gallery nights, the basketball, the tacos, the art stuff with the kids... none of it does anything for me anymore. I just show up, feel absolutely nothing, and then try to figure out how to leave without being rude. 

Speaking of being rude, I actually bumped into Mr. Peterson at the grocery store last week. He was in the produce section, squeezing every single avocado like he was inspecting diamonds. I almost didn't recognize him because he grew this huge, bushy beard. It made him look like a lumberjack or something. He had this plaid shirt on, too, which really completed the look. My uncle used to wear plaid shirts all the time when he worked at the lumber yard up in Oregon. He sent me a postcard from there once, and it had a picture of this giant Paul Bunyan statue. I always thought Paul Bunyan was a weird story. Like, a giant lumberjack with a blue ox? Who comes up with that stuff? Probably someone who spent too much time in the woods. 

But anyway, yeah, to answer your question... no. No slightly better days. Just flat.

He actually started memorizing the saxophone solos because the track just kept looping over and over. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... no, it doesn't come and go. It's just always flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this flat feeling is something that you experience every single day without any variation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, man, like I keep saying... every single day. It's exactly the same. It's honestly like Groundhog Day. Did you ever see that movie? We watched it in my ninth-grade English class, right before I stopped going to school. My teacher, Mr. Peterson, he was completely obsessed with Bill Murray. He had this giant Ghostbusters poster right behind his desk, but it was the one from the sequel with the ghost holding up two fingers. I never really got the hype with Ghostbusters, honestly. The effects look so goofy now, like old claymation. My buddy Terry tried to argue that practical effects are way better than CGI, and he actually spent like three hundred dollars on this vintage animatronic zombie prop for Halloween just to prove a point. It broke the second he plugged it in, just started smoking in his living room. It smelled like burnt hair in his apartment for a week. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I'm pretty sure I already told you, but there's no variation at all. Whether it's shooting hoops, going to the downtown galleries, getting those tacos, or doing the art projects with the neighborhood kids... I don't get excited about a single bit of it anymore. I just stand around feeling totally empty until I can sneak off and go home.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two months where you felt even slightly better than the others?\n\nPatient: I feel like a broken record here, doc, but no. Not even slightly better. It's just... two months, two years, it doesn't matter. It's that same flat feeling. Like I said, the gallery nights, the basketball, the tacos, the art stuff with the kids... none of it does anything for me anymore. I just show up, feel absolutely nothing, and then try to figure out how to leave without being rude. \n\nSpeaking of being rude, I actually bumped into Mr. Peterson at the grocery store last week. He was in the produce section, squeezing every single avocado like he was inspecting diamonds. I almost didn't recognize him because he grew this huge, bushy beard. It made him look like a lumberjack or something. He had this plaid shirt on, too, which really completed the look. My uncle used to wear plaid shirts all the time when he worked at the lumber yard up in Oregon. He sent me a postcard from there once, and it had a picture of this giant Paul Bunyan statue. I always thought Paul Bunyan was a weird story. Like, a giant lumberjack with a blue ox? Who comes up with that stuff? Probably someone who spent too much time in the woods. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, to answer your question... no. No slightly better days. Just flat.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I honestly don't know if it'll ever go away. I mean, I hope so, but... I don't know. Like I keep telling you, man, it's just completely wiped out all the good stuff. It smelled like burnt hair in his apartment for a week. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I'm pretty sure I already told you, but there's no variation at all. Whether it's shooting hoops, going to the downtown galleries, getting those tacos, or doing the art projects with the neighborhood kids... I don't get excited about a single bit of it anymore. I just stand around feeling totally empty until I can sneak off and go home.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two months where you felt even slightly better than the others?\n\nPatient: I feel like a broken record here, doc, but no. Not even slightly better. It's just... two months, two years, it doesn't matter. It's that same flat feeling. Like I said, the gallery nights, the basketball, the tacos, the art stuff with the kids... none of it does anything for me anymore. I just show up, feel absolutely nothing, and then try to figure out how to leave without being rude. \n\nSpeaking of being rude, I actually bumped into Mr. Peterson at the grocery store last week. He was in the produce section, squeezing every single avocado like he was inspecting diamonds. I almost didn't recognize him because he grew this huge, bushy beard. It made him look like a lumberjack or something. He had this plaid shirt on, too, which really completed the look. My uncle used to wear plaid shirts all the time when he worked at the lumber yard up in Oregon. He sent me a postcard from there once, and it had a picture of this giant Paul Bunyan statue. I always thought Paul Bunyan was a weird story. Like, a giant lumberjack with a blue ox? Who comes up with that stuff? Probably someone who spent too much time in the woods. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, to answer your question... no. No slightly better days. Just flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this flat feeling you're experiencing might ever go away on its own?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know if it'll ever go away. I mean, I hope so, but... I don't know. Like I keep telling you, man, it's just completely wiped out all the good stuff. The gallery nights, playing basketball, grabbing those food-truck tacos, helping the neighborhood kids with their art... I used to get so pumped for all of that. And now I just stand there feeling totally flat and try to come up with an excuse to leave. \n\nBailing is practically an art form for me now. Terry—that coworker I was telling you about—he's the absolute master of bailing, though. We were working the floor at the shop the other day, folding these graphic tees, and this customer comes up asking about the thread count on a novelty t-shirt. Like, who asks about thread count on a ten-dollar shirt with a cartoon hotdog on it? Anyway, Terry just points at the ceiling, says \"Code Silver,\" and walks straight into the stockroom. He stayed back there for like forty minutes organizing shoe boxes by color. He's got this weird system where he puts the blue boxes next to the red ones because he thinks it looks patriotic or something. Honestly, the stockroom is a disaster anyway. The fluorescent lights back there hum so loud, it's super annoying. The manager says he's gonna get an electrician to look at it, but he never does. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't think so, no. I mean, I can't remember ever feeling this... this empty before. Like, I know I sound like a broken record, but I used to genuinely get so excited about things. The gallery nights, playing basketball, getting those food-truck tacos, helping the neighborhood kids with their art projects... I loved all that stuff. Now I just stand there feeling completely flat, and I usually end up making some excuse to leave early. 

It's actually getting kind of hard to come up with new excuses. I used the "I left my stove on" one the other day, which is stupid because I don't even cook. I basically live on microwave meals and those little pre-packaged cheese sticks. The ones with the red wax? My mom used to put those in my lunchbox when I was a kid. She'd always draw a little smiley face on the wax with a Sharpie. She had this whole drawer in the kitchen just for Sharpies, like every color you could imagine. Neon pink, metallic silver... she even had this one that smelled like blueberries. I don't know why they make scented markers, honestly. It just makes kids want to eat them. My cousin Joey actually ate a whole green marker once when he was like five. His tongue was green for a week, and his mom freaked out and called poison control. Poison control is such an intense job, right? Like, you just sit by a phone all day waiting for people to tell you what weird thing they swallowed. 

Anyway... yeah, I've never had a time like this before where I just don't care about anything for this long. It's totally new.
